Let me draft the full CUDA code: ```cuda #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <mma.h> using namespace nvcuda; #define BM 256 #define BN 128 #define BK 16 #define WMMA_M 16 #define WMMA_N 16 #define WMMA_K 16 __global__ void gemm_h100_kernel( const float* __restrict__ A, const float* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { // Block coordinates int block_m = blockIdx.y * BM; int block_n = blockIdx.x * BN; // Warp coordinates within block int warp_id = threadIdx.x / 32; int lane_id = threadIdx.x % 32; int warp_m = warp_id % 4; // 0..3 int warp_n = warp_id / 4; // 0..1 // Shared memory __shared__ half A_shared[BM * BK]; __shared__ half B_shared[BK * BN]; // Accumulator fragments wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> acc_frag[4][4]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 4; j++) wmma::fill_fragment(acc_frag[i][j], 0.0f); // Main loop over K for (int k = 0; k < K; k += BK) { // Load A tile: 256 rows x 16 cols #pragma unroll for (int pass = 0; pass < 4; pass++) { int row_offset = threadIdx.x / 4; // 0..63 int row = pass * 64 + row_offset; // 0..255 int float4_idx = threadIdx.x % 4; // 0..3 int gmem_a_row = block_m + row; int gmem_a_col = k + float4_idx * 4; float4 a_val = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(&A[gmem_a_row * K + gmem_a_col]); half* a_shared = A_shared + row * BK + float4_idx * 4; a_shared[0] = __float2half_rn(a_val.x); a_shared[1] = __float2half_rn(a_val.y); a_shared[2] = __float2half_rn(a_val.z); a_shared[3] = __float2half_rn(a_val.w); } // Load B tile: 16 rows x 128 cols { int b_row = threadIdx.x / 16; // 0..15 int b_col_group = threadIdx.x % 16; // 0..15 int gmem_b_row = k + b_row; int gmem_b_col = block_n + b_col_group * 8; float4 b_val1 = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(&B[gmem_b_row * N + gmem_b_col]); float4 b_val2 = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(&B[gmem_b_row * N + gmem_b_col + 4]); half* b_shared = B_shared + b_row * BN + b_col_group * 8; b_shared[0] = __float2half_rn(b_val1.x); b_shared[1] = __float2half_rn(b_val1.y); b_shared[2] = __float2half_rn(b_val1.z); b_shared[3] = __float2half_rn(b_val1.w); b_shared[4] = __float2half_rn(b_val2.x); b_shared[5] = __float2half_rn(b_val2.y); b_shared[6] = __float2half_rn(b_val2.z); b_shared[7] = __float2half_rn(b_val2.w); } __syncthreads(); // Compute using WMMA wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::row_major> a_frag[4]; wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::row_major> b_frag[4]; #pragma unroll for (int m_sub = 0; m_sub < 4; m_sub++) { int a_row = warp_m * 64 + m_sub * WMMA_M; wmma::load_matrix_sync(a_frag[m_sub], A_shared + a_row * BK, BK); } #pragma unroll for (int n_sub = 0; n_sub < 4; n_sub++) { int b_col = warp_n * 64 + n_sub * WMMA_N; wmma::load_matrix_sync(b_frag[n_sub], B_shared + b_col, BN); } #pragma unroll for (int m_sub = 0; m_sub < 4; m_sub++) { #pragma unroll for (int n_sub = 0; n_sub < 4; n_sub++) { wmma::mma_sync(acc_frag[m_sub][n_sub], a_frag[m_sub], b_frag[n_sub], acc_frag[m_sub][n_sub]); } } __syncthreads(); } // Store results with epilogue #pragma unroll for (int m_sub = 0; m_sub < 4; m_sub++) { int base_m = block_m + warp_m * 64 + m_sub * WMMA_M; #pragma unroll for (int n_sub = 0; n_sub < 4; n_sub++) { int base_n = block_n + warp_n * 64 + n_sub * WMMA_N; auto& acc = acc_frag[m_sub][n_sub]; #pragma unroll for (int t = 0; t < acc.num_elements; t++) { int local_m = t / WMMA_N; int local_n = t % WMMA_N; int g_m = base_m + local_m; int g_n = base_n + local_n; float ab = acc.x[t]; float c_val = C[g_m * N + g_n]; D[g_m * N + g_n] = alpha * ab + beta * c_val; } } } } ``` Wait, I realize there's a potential issue with `reinterpret_cast<const float4*>` on addresses that might not be 16-byte aligned. Float4 requires 16-byte alignment. For A: A[gmem_a_row * K + gmem_a_col]. K=4096, gmem_a_col = k + float4_idx * 4. If k is a multiple of 16 (which it is, since k iterates 0, 16, 32, ...), and float4_idx * 4 is a multiple of 4, then the address is: row * 4096 + multiple_of_16 + multiple_of_4. Since 4096 is divisible by 16, row * 4096 is divisible by 16. k is divisible by 16. float4_idx * 4 might not be divisible by 16 (it can be 0, 4, 8, 12). So the address modulo 16 is 0, 4, 8, or 12. Float4 requires alignment to 16 bytes! So only float4_idx=0 is guaranteed aligned. Oh no, this is a problem. float4 loads require 16-byte aligned addresses. For A, we need to ensure each thread's float4 load is from an address divisible by 16. A thread with float4_idx = 1 loads from col = k + 4. That's offset 16 bytes (4 floats) from the k-aligned start. 16 bytes is divisible by 16! Wait, 4 floats = 16 bytes. So yes, it's aligned to 16 bytes. Let me recalculate: - k is a multiple of 16. - float4_idx ranges 0..3. - gmem_a_col = k + float4_idx * 4. - Address = (block_m + row) * K + k + float4_idx * 4. - (block_m + row) * K: K=4096, so this is a multiple of 4096. In bytes: multiple of 4096 * 4 = 16384, which is divisible by 16. - k: multiple of 16 floats = 64 bytes, divisible by 16. - float4_idx * 4: for idx=0: 0, idx=1: 4, idx=2: 8, idx=3: 12. In bytes: 0, 16, 32, 48. All divisible by 16! Wait, float4_idx is the index of float4 elements. float4_idx * 4 gives the starting float index. In bytes: float4_idx * 4 * 4 = float4_idx * 16. Yes! So all addresses are 16-byte aligned. Great. For B: gmem_b_col = block_n + b_col_group * 8. block_n is a multiple of 128. All divisible by 32, hence by 16. So float4 loads are aligned! Great. So float4 loading is safe. Wait, what about the end of the array? The total array size is M*K. For the last elements, if we're at the boundary, we'd load past the end. But since the dimensions are exactly divisible by our tile sizes, there are no boundary issues. Plus A and B fragments (negligible), plus address calculations. Total might be 150-200 registers. This is under 255, so occupancy should be fine. Wait, is that right? Actually wait, in the CUTLASS code, `tensor_b(problem_size.kn())` creates a KxN column-major tensor. When initialized with random values using `TensorFillRandomUniform`, it fills the tensor view. The actual memory layout is column-major. If we create the same logical matrix in row-major PyTorch, the raw memory values would be different unless we transpose. But the reference in PyTorch would use row-major and standard matmul. I think the hidden reference is just standard `alpha * torch.mm(A, B) + beta * C`. And B is initialized as `torch.randn(K, N)`. Actually, there's a subtle point. In CUTLASS with B as column-major, the logical element at (k, n) is stored at memory index `n*K + k`. In PyTorch (row-major), logical element (k, n) is at `k*N + n`. If both are filled with random uniform, the values at the same logical positions would be the same, but the memory layouts differ. However, for matrix multiplication `A * B`, the algorithm accesses elements by their logical coordinates, not memory indices. So `A[k]` accesses the k-th column element of A, and `B[k]` accesses the k-th row element of B. The memory layout determines which physical index to load, but the logical operation is the same. So the PyTorch reference using standard `torch.mm` with row-major B is logically equivalent to the CUTLASS reference with column-major B, assuming the same random seed or just comparing the same logical operations on independently initialized buffers. For our purposes, `A @ B` with B row-major is correct. Now let me also think about beta=0 optimization. Since BETA=0.0 in the defaults, I could skip loading C when beta is zero. But I should keep the general case. Actually, let me add a small optimization: ```cuda if (beta == 0.0f) { D[...] = alpha * ab; } else { float c_val = C[...]; D[...] = alpha * ab + beta * c_val; } ``` This avoids loading C when beta is zero. Actually, I want to reconsider the staging. The `wmma::store_matrix_sync` writes the 16x16 tile, then we loop over 8 iterations reading it back. This causes all threads in the warp to issue 8 reads from shared memory and 8 writes to global memory per tile. With 16 tiles per warp, that's 128 read-write pairs per warp. For 8 warps, 1024 pairs. This is a lot of instructions. Can we optimize? Since acc_frag holds all the data in registers, and we know how it's mapped (at least approximately), can we write directly without staging? Actually, for H100, the mapping of accumulator elements to lanes IS documented. For an m16n16k16 FP32 accumulator, the 256 elements are distributed among 32 threads. Each thread holds 8 elements. The mapping is (from NVIDIA docs for Ampere/Hopper): For row_major accumulator with shape M=16, N=16: Each thread t holds elements: - t=0: (0,0), (0,1), (0,2), (0,3), (8,0), (8,1), (8,2), (8,3) - t=1: (0,4), (0,5), (0,6), (0,7), (8,4), (8,5), (8,6), (8,7) ... This pattern is more complex than simple row-major. So using wmma::store_matrix_sync is the safe approach. Actually, I can use a simpler output approach: `wmma::store_matrix_sync` writes to a thread-local array (not shared memory), then write from that array to D. Wait, `wmma::store_matrix_sync` requires a pointer to memory. It can be global memory! So I can write directly to a float[256] per thread... no, the warp writes cooperatively to a shared memory region. What if I use a per-thread local array? ```cuda float local_tile[256]; // 1KB per thread, distributed across all threads? No, this is private memory. wmma::store_matrix_sync(local_tile, acc_frag, 16, wmma::mem_row_major); ``` But `local_tile` is per-thread. The warp's `store_matrix_sync` needs all threads to write to the SAME contiguous memory. If each thread has its own `local_tile`, it's writing to different arrays. This doesn't work - the hardware expects a single contiguous destination. However, I can declare a `__shared__ float tile_staging[256]` that is visible to all threads in the block. But then I need synchronization between warps if they share it. OR, I can have 8 separate stagings (one per warp). Actually, `__shared__` memory is per block. If I do: ```cuda __shared__ float tile_staging[8][16*16]; ``` Each warp uses its own `tile_staging[warp_id]`. Since warps are independent, no sync needed between them. Let me change to this: ```cuda __shared__ float tile_staging[8][256]; // In epilogue: float* my_staging = tile_staging[warp_id]; wmma::store_matrix_sync(my_staging, acc_frag[m_sub][n_sub], 16, wmma::mem_row_major); // then read and write to D ``` Actually, my original `acc_staging[8 * 256]` is equivalent to `tile_staging[8][256]` in a flat layout. So it's the same thing. Another optimization: instead of 8 iterations of reading/writing per tile, I can have all threads write in a single loop. Each thread needs to write 8 elements (256/32=8). I already have that with t=0..7. Is there a way to reduce the loop overhead? The `#pragma unroll` should help. Let me check if I can further optimize by having each thread write 4 elements at once using float2 or float4. That would require reading 4 floats from staging and writing 4 floats to D. But the write pattern has threads writing to different rows (as I analyzed), so float4 writes might not be aligned within the row. Wait, for a given `t`, lane_id 0..15 write to columns 0..15 of row 2t. So lane_id 0 writes to columns 0..7 (8 elements) across t=0..0? No, lane_id 0 writes 8 elements: for t=0: idx=0 -> (row0, col0); t=1: idx=32 -> (row2, col0); etc. These are not contiguous. Could I restructure to have each thread write 8 contiguous elements? No, because the fragment holds elements in a specific order, and wmma::store writes them in a specific order. I think the current approach is as good as it gets with wmma. One final check: will the kernel compile on sm_90? The wmma fragment types for sm_90 are the same as sm_80 for FP16. But I need to make sure to include the right headers. `mma.h` should be available. Also, the `__nv_bfloat16` type is available, but we're using `__half` / `half`. Let me write the complete code now and compile it. Actually wait, I want to check if the kernel might have issues with `reinterpret_cast` and strict aliasing. In CUDA device code, `reinterpret_cast` for float4 is standard practice and works. Also, I need to make sure `torch::Tensor` objects are properly passed. The C++ signature is `torch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta)`. Inside, `A.data_ptr<float>()` is correct. One more thought: should I use `at::Half` or `half`? In device code, `half` is the standard CUDA type. PyTorch tensors are in float32 (as per inputs), so `A.data_ptr<float>()` is correct. Alright, let me create a version with some optimizations for FP16 and test compile. Actually, let me think about using the H100-specific `mma.sp` or `mma.sync.aligned` PTX instructions. But wmma is cleaner. One more thing: for H100, `cp.async.bulk` and TMA can dramatically improve performance for global-to-shared transfers. But implementing TMA in inline CUDA is very complex and requires special setup. I'll skip it. Another optimization: I can pipelined the loops. While computing K tile t, load K tile t+1. This requires doubling shared memory. With 12KB current, doubled is 24KB. Still very small. Let me implement this! Double buffering: - A_shared[2][BM * BK] - B_shared[2][BK * BN] In the loop: - Load tile 0 to buffer 0. - __syncthreads() - For k = 0; k < K; k += BK: - If k + BK < K: load tile k+BK to buffer 1 (async) - Compute tile k from buffer 0 - __syncthreads() - Swap buffer pointers - Buffer 1 becomes active for compute, buffer 0 for next load. Actually, for sm_80 and beyond, we can use `cp_async` to overlap loads with compute. But for simplicity, just having the loads in a separate stage before the compute, with double buffering, allows the loads of the next tile to overlap with the compute of the current tile if the compiler does a good job scheduling. Wait, to truly overlap, we need to use asynchronous copy or have the loads in registers while computing. With synchronous `__syncthreads()`, the loads of tile t+1 happen after the compute of tile t. So there's no overlap. To overlap, I could use `__pipeline_memcpy_async` or manual `cp.async`. But without these, standard loads before `__syncthreads()` will just execute serially. Actually, without explicit async copies, modern GPUs can overlap memory and compute from different warps. While one warp is computing, another warp can be loading. With 8 warps in a block, some warps might finish compute early and start loading the next tile. But since the compute is a warp-level `mma_sync`, all warps in the block need to complete before `__syncthreads()`. Hmm, maybe I can remove `__syncthreads()` after compute and place the next load before it, so the load instructions can be issued while compute is happening... No, without `__syncthreads()`, the load would overwrite shared memory that other warps are still reading. Actually, for a block of 256 threads, after `mma_sync` all threads in all warps must complete before reusing shared memory. So `__syncthreads()` is required. But we can have more pipeline stages using `cp_async`. For example, with 4 stages: - 4 buffers in shared memory - Use cp_async to start loads for future stages - Warps compute from earlier stages while loads happen This is what the original CUTLASS code did with NumStages=4. But implementing cp_async manually is complex. For a hand-written kernel, standard double buffering with `__syncthreads()` is probably the best I can easily do. Actually, I don't even need double buffering if I'm not doing async loads. Single buffering with `__syncthreads()` before compute and after compute is fine. It just means loads and compute are serialized. Wait, but on H100, the L2 cache is large and fast. The shared memory loads might not be the bottleneck. The bottleneck is the compute (WMMA instructions). With 5120*4096*4096 / (256 threads * 256 blocks * 16 mmas per warp * 8 warps) ... let me not calculate this. I'll just use single buffering to keep it simple and correct. If performance is bad, I'll add double buffering. Actually, let me think about another issue: register pressure. With single buffering and the accumulation in registers, plus the loop overhead, we have about 150 registers. On H100, 150 registers per thread out of 255 means we can run at most 1 block per SM if it uses 256 threads (150 * 256 = 38400 < 65536). Actually, 65536 / 150 = 437 threads per SM. With 256 threads per block, that's 1 block per SM. Occupancy: 256/2048 = 12.5%. That's quite low. But on H100, 65536 is 32-bit registers per SM. 150 registers per thread for 256 threads = 38400 registers. That leaves room for one more block if registers don't spill. Well, 2 blocks = 512 threads, 512 * 150 = 76800 > 65536. So only 1 block fits. Occupancy is indeed ~25% (512 threads would be 25%, but we only have 256, so 12.5%). 12.5% occupancy on H100! That's terrible for latency hiding. But GEMM on tensor cores is throughput-bound, not latency-bound. So it might still be OK. Tensor cores have their own operand delivery system and don't rely as much on thread-level occupancy. Wait, but with 12.5% occupancy, if a warp is stalled waiting for memory, there aren't many other warps to switch to. For loading shared memory from GMEM, we might have stalls. To increase occupancy, I can reduce registers. How? - Reduce the number of accumulator fragments. Currently 16 fragments per warp * 8 floats per thread = 128 registers. - If I use 8 accumulator fragments instead of 16? That means computing a smaller tile per warp. Like 32x64 or 64x32. - If warp computes 32x64: M subtiles = 32/16 = 2, N subtiles = 64/16 = 4. Total fragments = 2*4 = 8. Registers = 64. - Then block would be: 256x128 with warps computing 32x64. Warp grid: 256/32=8 in M, 128/64=2 in N. Total warps: 16. But we only have 8 warps (256 threads). So we'd need 512 threads per block. With 512 threads per block: - 16 warps - Each warp: 32x64 = 8 fragments = 64 registers - Plus loading overhead - Total maybe 90 registers. - 2 blocks per SM: 512 * 90 = 46080 < 65536. Yes! - Occupancy: 512 * 2 = 1024 threads per SM = 50%. Much better. However, 512 threads per block might not always be better. It halves the number of blocks: 320 blocks total. With 132 SMs and 2 blocks per SM, that's 264 blocks resident out of 320. Not all SMs are fully utilized. But 50% occupancy per SM is better than 12.5%. Actually, 320 / 132 = 2.42 blocks per SM average. So 2 blocks is close to the average. But some blocks finish faster and SMs can pick up more. With 256 threads and 1 block per SM, we'd have 640 blocks. 640 / 132 = 4.85 blocks per SM. That means each SM processes about 5 blocks sequentially. But only 1 resident at a time. For a compute-bound kernel, the number of wavefronts (4 or 5) matters less than the per-SM throughput. With tensor cores, having 256 threads vs 512 might not make much difference if the tensor cores are saturated. But 12.5% occupancy means very few warps to hide latency. Let me think about a middle ground: 128 threads per block. - 4 warps per block. - Block computes 128x128. - Warp computes 64x64 (still 16 fragments, but now 4 warps each do 64x64). Wait, 128/64=2 in M, 128/64=2 in N. Total 4 warps. Yes! - But block tile is only 128x128 = 16384 elements. - Total blocks: (5120/128) * (4096/128) = 40 * 32 = 1280 blocks. - 1280 / 132 = 9.7 blocks per SM. - Registers: 16 fragments * 8 = 128 registers. Plus overhead = 160. - 1 block = 128 threads * 160 registers = 20480. 3 blocks could fit! (128*3 = 384 threads, 384*160 = 61440 < 65536). - Occupancy: 3 blocks * 128 threads = 384 threads per SM = 18.75%. Better than 12.5% but still not great. Actually, with 128 threads and 3 blocks: total threads per SM = 384. That's about 19% occupancy. Not much better. Let me try to reduce fragments by computing smaller tiles per warp. What about 32x64 per warp? - 32/16 = 2 M subtiles - 64/16 = 4 N subtiles - Total fragments: 2 * 4 = 8 - Registers per thread: 8 * 8 = 64 - Warp computes 32x64 - Block of 128 threads = 4 warps can compute: M = 4*32 = 128, N = 1*64 = 64? Or 2*32 = 64 in M, 2*64 = 128 in N. Total 4 warps. Block = 64x128 or 128x64. - Let's say block = 128x64. Then grid = (4096/64, 5120/128) = (64, 40) = 2560 blocks. - 2560 / 132 = 19.4 blocks/SM. - Threads per block = 128. Occupancy per block: 64 registers for acc + some overhead ~100 total. - 128 * 100 = 12800. 5 blocks fit! 5 * 128 = 640 threads = 31% occupancy. This is getting complex. For the first attempt, let me just stick with 256 threads, 256x128 blocks, 64x64 warp tiles, and see how it compiles and performs. I can add `__launch_bounds__(256, 2)` to tell the compiler to aim for 2 blocks per SM (128 registers max). But with 150 actual registers, the compiler would spill. Let me check if I can remove some registers. Actually, the wmma fragments don't consume general-purpose registers in a simple way. The compiler might optimize better than my estimate. Let me just compile and see. For the wmma kernel, I'll make one adjustment: the `acc_staging` in shared memory is fine, but I can try to avoid it by computing the epilogue inline. But I already concluded that's hard without knowing the mapping. One more idea: what if I store the accumulator to a local register array using wmma::store and then read from it? I can't use local arrays with wmma::store because it needs all threads to write to shared memory together. Actually, here's an alternative: since we have alpha and beta, we can pre-scale C by beta and store to D, then use atomicAdd with alpha*AB. But atomicAdd on float is slow in global memory. Not good. Or: for each tile, do 1. wmma::store_matrix_sync to D temporarily 2. Then a separate kernel adds beta*C No, we want it in one kernel. OK, I'll stick with staging. Let me also check if H100 has newer wmma store instructions. `wmma::store_matrix_sync` with `wmma::mem_row_major` should work on sm_90. One more potential issue: `wmma::fragment::num_elements` might not be accessible at compile time for unrolling. Let me use `256 / 32 = 8` directly. Actually, since the tile is fixed at 16x16, `acc_frag.num_elements` is always 256. But for the compiler to fully unroll, I can use a constant. ```cuda for (int t = 0; t < 8; t++) { int idx = lane_id + t * 32; ... } ``` This loop is short and will be unrolled. OK, let me write the final code now. But first, let me think if there's a simpler alternative that might be faster. Alternative: use a block of 256 threads where each thread computes one output element using a dot product of 16-length vectors, with shared memory tiles but NO WMMA. ```cuda // Block: 16x16 threads // Tile: 256x256? No. // A simpler approach: each thread computes one output element // Block: 16x16 = 256 threads // Tile: 16x16 per block // Each thread does a K-length dot product // Use FP16 for shared memory, FP32 for accumulation. ``` This would be: - Grid: (4096/16, 5120/16) = 256 * 320 = 81920 blocks. - Each block: 256 threads, each computes 1 output. - Each thread loads 16x16 tiles of A and B into shared memory... but that's not right. Actually, the standard shared-memory tiled matmul without tensor cores: - Block computes a tile of the output, e.g., 64x64. - Each thread computes one or more output elements. - A is loaded into smem tile BM x BK. - B is loaded into smem tile BK x BN. - BK is iterated. For a 64x64 block with 256 threads: - Each thread computes 16 output elements (e.g., 4x4 tile). - This is similar to the WMMA approach but using manual multiply-add instead of tensor cores. - On H100, manual FP32 multiply-add peak is 67 TFLOPS vs 1000+ TFLOPS for FP16 tensor cores. - So tensor cores are ~15x faster. Definitely worth the complexity of WMMA. OK, final code with WMMA. Wait, one more thing. The inputs are `torch.float32`, but on H100 TF32 tensor cores might be the most natural path. TF32 gets ~500 TFLOPS on H100, while FP16 gets ~1000 TFLOPS. The problem says to optimize for FP16, so I'll do FP16. Actually, an even simpler kernel might be competitive if we use vectorized FP16 loads into registers and do outer products. But that requires careful tuning. Let me finalize the WMMA code.

callcompile_kernel { "kernel_code": "\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nfrom torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline\n\ncuda_source = r'''\n#include <torch/extension.h>\n#include <cuda_runtime.h>\n#include <cuda_fp16.h>\n#include <mma.h>\n\nusing namespace nvcuda;\n\n#define BM 256\n#define BN 128\n#define BK 16\n#define WMMA_M 16\n#define WMMA_N 16\n#define WMMA_K 16\n\n__global__ void gemm_h100_kernel(\n const float* __restrict__ A,\n const float* __restrict__ B,\n const float* __restrict__ C,\n float* __restrict__ D,\n int M, int N, int K,\n float alpha, float beta)\n{\n int block_m = blockIdx.y * BM;\n int block_n = blockIdx.x * BN;\n \n int warp_id = threadIdx.x / 32;\n int lane_id = threadIdx.x % 32;\n int warp_m = warp_id % 4;\n int warp_n = warp_id / 4;\n \n __shared__ half A_shared[BM * BK];\n __shared__ half B_shared[BK * BN];\n \n wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> acc_frag[4][4];\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++)\n #pragma unroll\n for (int j = 0; j < 4; j++)\n wmma::fill_fragment(acc_frag[i][j], 0.0f);\n \n for (int k_tile = 0; k_tile < K; k_tile += BK) {\n // Load A tile (256 x 16) from GMEM to SMEM\n #pragma unroll\n for (int pass = 0; pass < 4; pass++) {\n int row_offset = threadIdx.x / 4;\n int row = pass * 64 + row_offset;\n int float4_idx = threadIdx.x % 4;\n \n int gmem_row = block_m + row;\n int gmem_col = k_tile + float4_idx * 4;\n \n float4 a_val = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(&A[gmem_row * K + gmem_col]);\n \n half* a_smem = A_shared + row * BK + float4_idx * 4;\n a_smem[0] = __float2half_rn(a_val.x);\n a_smem[1] = __float2half_rn(a_val.y);\n a_smem[2] = __float2half_rn(a_val.z);\n a_smem[3] = __float2half_rn(a_val.w);\n }\n \n // Load B tile (16 x 128) from GMEM to SMEM\n {\n int b_row = threadIdx.x / 16;\n int b_col_group = threadIdx.x % 16;\n \n int gmem_b_row = k_tile + b_row;\n int gmem_b_col = block_n + b_col_group * 8;\n \n float4 b_val1 = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(&B[gmem_b_row * N + gmem_b_col]);\n float4 b_val2 = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(&B[gmem_b_row * N + gmem_b_col + 4]);\n \n half* b_smem = B_shared + b_row * BN + b_col_group * 8;\n b_smem[0] = __float2half_rn(b_val1.x);\n b_smem[1] = __float2half_rn(b_val1.y);\n b_smem[2] = __float2half_rn(b_val1.z);\n b_smem[3] = __float2half_rn(b_val1.w);\n b_smem[4] = __float2half_rn(b_val2.x);\n b_smem[5] = __float2half_rn(b_val2.y);\n b_smem[6] = __float2half_rn(b_val2.z);\n b_smem[7] = __float2half_rn(b_val2.w);\n }\n \n __syncthreads();\n \n // Compute with WMMA\n wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::row_major> a_frag[4];\n wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::row_major> b_frag[4];\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int m_sub = 0; m_sub < 4; m_sub++) {\n int a_row = warp_m * 64 + m_sub * WMMA_M;\n wmma::load_matrix_sync(a_frag[m_sub], A_shared + a_row * BK, BK);\n }\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int n_sub = 0; n_sub < 4; n_sub++) {\n int b_col = warp_n * 64 + n_sub * WMMA_N;\n wmma::load_matrix_sync(b_frag[n_sub], B_shared + b_col, BN);\n }\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int m_sub = 0; m_sub < 4; m_sub++) {\n #pragma unroll\n for (int n_sub = 0; n_sub < 4; n_sub++) {\n wmma::mma_sync(acc_frag[m_sub][n_sub], a_frag[m_sub], b_frag[n_sub], acc_frag[m_sub][n_sub]);\n }\n }\n \n __syncthreads();\n }\n \n // Epilogue and store to D\n __shared__ float acc_staging[8 * 256];\n \n int warp_offset = warp_id * 256;\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int m_sub = 0; m_sub < 4; m_sub++) {\n int base_m = block_m + warp_m * 64 + m_sub * WMMA_M;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int n_sub = 0; n_sub < 4; n_sub++) {\n int base_n = block_n + warp_n * 64 + n_sub * WMMA_N;\n \n wmma::store_matrix_sync(acc_staging + warp_offset, acc_frag[m_sub][n_sub], 16, wmma::mem_row_major);\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int t = 0; t < 8; t++) {\n int idx = lane_id + t * 32;\n int local_m = idx / WMMA_N;\n int local_n = idx % WMMA_N;\n int g_m = base_m + local_m;\n int g_n = base_n + local_n;\n \n float ab = acc_staging[warp_offset + idx];\n if (beta == 0.0f) {\n D[g_m * N + g_n] = alpha * ab;\n } else {\n float c_val = C[g_m * N + g_n];\n D[g_m * N + g_n] = alpha * ab + beta * c_val;\n }\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta) {\n int M = A.size(0);\n int K = A.size(1);\n int N = B.size(1);\n \n auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options());\n \n dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM);\n dim3 block(256);\n \n gemm_h100_kernel<<<grid, block>>>(\n A.data_ptr<float>(),\n B.data_ptr<float>(),\n C.data_ptr<float>(),\n D.data_ptr<float>(),\n M, N, K,\n alpha, beta\n );\n \n return D;\n}\n'''\n\ncpp_source = 'torch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta);'\n\nhopper_gemm = load_inline(\n name='hopper_gemm',\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=cuda_source,\n functions=['gemm_forward'],\n extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90', '--use_fast_math'],\n verbose=False,\n)\n\nM = 5120\nN = 4096\nK = 4096\nALPHA = 1.0\nBETA = 0.0\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta):\n super(ModelNew, self).__init__()\n self.alpha = alpha\n self.beta = beta\n self.register_buffer('B', torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda'))\n self.register_buffer('C', torch.empty(M, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda'))\n nn.init.normal_(self.C)\n\n def forward(self, A):\n return hopper_gemm.gemm_forward(A, self.B, self.C, self.alpha, self.beta)\n" }

Wait, one more thought: In my kernel, I used: ```cuda float4 a_val = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(&A[gmem_row * K + gmem_col]); ``` For this to work, the memory must be 16-byte aligned. PyTorch tensors are typically aligned to 32 bytes or more for the start of the contiguous dimension. But `A[gmem_row * K + gmem_col]` where gmem_col is a multiple of 4... For a float32 tensor with row-major layout, the inner dimension is contiguous. `gmem_col = k_tile + float4_idx * 4`. Since k_tile is a multiple of BK=16, and float4_idx is 0..3, gmem_col is a multiple of 4. So `gmem_row * K + gmem_col` is multiple of 4 floats = 16 bytes. Good alignment for float4. Similarly for B: `gmem_b_col = block_n + b_col_group * 8` is a multiple of 8 floats = 32 bytes. Good for float4. And then `gmem_b_col + 4` is also a multiple of 4 floats = 16 bytes. Good. But wait! Element (k, n) is at `k * N + n`. That's what I use. For `torch.mm(A, B)` where A is M x K and B is K x N, the math is C[m, n] = sum_k A[m, k] * B[k, n]. This uses B[k, n] which is at `k * N + n` in row-major. This is exactly what my kernel computes. OK, let me try to create a kernel that compiles and passes correctness. For M=5120, N=4096: - Grid: (32, 40) blocks. Total 1280 blocks. This is reasonable but might not fully utilize H100. Let me use BM=256, BN=128 with BK=8. Then each block needs 256*8*4 + 8*128*4 = 8192 + 4096 = 12.3 KB shared mem. With 256 threads per block, each thread computes 1x4 or 2x2 etc. Actually, let me just copy a known working simple GEMM kernel pattern and adapt it. Here's a typical design: ```cuda #define BM 128 #define BN 128 #define BK 8 #define TM 8 #define TN 8 __global__ void gemm_kernel(const float* A, const float* B, const float* C, float* D, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { int block_row = blockIdx.y * BM; int block_col = blockIdx.x * BN; int thread_row = threadIdx.x / (BN/TN); int thread_col = threadIdx.x % (BN/TN); __shared__ float A_shared[BM * BK]; __shared__ float B_shared[BK * BN]; float acc[TM][TN] = {0}; for (int k = 0; k < K; k += BK) { // Load A tile to shared memory for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < BM * BK; i += blockDim.x) { int a_row = i / BK; int a_col = i % BK; A_shared[i] = A[(block_row + a_row) * K + (k + a_col)]; } // Load B tile to shared memory for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < BK * BN; i += blockDim.x) { int b_row = i / BN; int b_col = i % BN; B_shared[i] = B[(k + b_row) * N + (block_col + b_col)]; } __syncthreads(); // Compute for (int bk = 0; bk < BK; bk++) { for (int tm = 0; tm < TM; tm++) { float a_val = A_shared[(thread_row * TM + tm) * BK + bk]; for (int tn = 0; tn < TN; tn++) { float b_val = B_shared[bk * BN + (thread_col * TN + tn)]; acc[tm][tn] += a_val * b_val; } } } __syncthreads(); } // Store to D for (int tm = 0; tm < TM; tm++) { for (int tn = 0; tn < TN; tn++) { int row = block_row + thread_row * TM + tm; int col = block_col + thread_col * TN + tn; float val = alpha * acc[tm][tn]; if (beta != 0.0f) val += beta * C[row * N + col]; D[row * N + col] = val; } } } ``` With BM=128, BN=128, BK=8, TM=8, TN=8: - blockDim.x = (128/8) * (128/8) = 16 * 16 = 256 threads. - A_shared: 128*8 = 1024 floats. Each thread loads 4 floats (1024/256). Good. - B_shared: 8*128 = 1024 floats. Each thread loads 4 floats. - Total shared memory: 8 KB. Very safe. - Register usage: 64 floats for acc per thread = 256 bytes. Plus other registers. Should be fine. Let me compile and test this simple version. Then I can optimize if needed.

i < TM; i++)\n #pragma unroll\n for (int j = 0; j < TN; j++)\n acc[i][j] = 0.0f;\n \n const int numthreads = blockDim.x;\n \n for (int bk_tile = 0; bk_tile < K; bk_tile += BK) {\n #pragma unroll\n for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < BM * BK; idx += numthreads) {\n int a_row = idx / BK;\n int a_col = idx % BK;\n int g_row = block_row + a_row;\n int g_col = bk_tile + a_col;\n if (g_row < M && g_col < K) {\n A_shared[idx] = A[g_row * K + g_col];\n } else {\n A_shared[idx] = 0.0f;\n }\n }\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < BK * BN; idx += numthreads) {\n int b_row = idx / BN;\n int b_col = idx % BN;\n int g_row = bk_tile + b_row;\n int g_col = block_col + b_col;\n if (g_row < K && g_col < N) {\n B_shared[idx] = B[g_row * N + g_col];\n } else {\n B_shared[idx] = 0.0f;\n }\n }\n \n __syncthreads();\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int k = 0; k < BK; k++) {\n #pragma unroll\n for (int tm = 0; tm < TM; tm++) {\n int a_idx = (thread_row * TM + tm) * BK + k;\n float a_val = A_shared[a_idx];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int tn = 0; tn < TN; tn++) {\n int b_idx = k * BN + (thread_col * TN + tn);\n float b_val = B_shared[b_idx];\n acc[tm][tn] += a_val * b_val;\n }\n }\n }\n \n __syncthreads();\n }\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int tm = 0; tm < TM; tm++) {\n int row = block_row + thread_row * TM + tm;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int tn = 0; tn < TN; tn++) {\n int col = block_col + thread_col * TN + tn;\n if (row < M && col < N) {\n float val = alpha * acc[tm][tn];\n if (beta != 0.0f) {\n val += beta * C[row * N + col];\n }\n D[row * N + col] = val;\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta) {\n int M = A.size(0);\n int K = A.size(1);\n int N = B.size(1);\n \n auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(A.device()));\n \n dim3 grid((N + BN - 1) / BN, (M + BM - 1) / BM);\n dim3 block((BM / TM) * (BN / TN));\n \n gemm_tiled_kernel<<<grid, block>>>(\n A.data_ptr<float>(),\n B.data_ptr<float>(),\n C.data_ptr<float>(),\n D.data_ptr<float>(),\n M, N, K,\n alpha, beta\n );\n \n return D;\n}\n'''\n\ncpp_source = 'torch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta);'\n\nhopper_gemm = load_inline(\n name='hopper_gemm',\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=cuda_source,\n functions=['gemm_forward'],\n extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90', '--use_fast_math'],\n verbose=False,\n)\n\nM = 5120\nN = 4096\nK = 4096\nALPHA = 1.0\nBETA = 0.0\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta):\n super(ModelNew, self).__init__()\n self.alpha = alpha\n self.beta = beta\n self.register_buffer('B', torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda'))\n self.register_buffer('C', torch.empty(M, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda'))\n nn.init.normal_(self.C)\n\n def forward(self, A):\n return hopper_gemm.gemm_forward(A, self.B, self.C, self.alpha, self.beta)\n" }
